It's a huge fire hazard, honestly. If there was an emergency, you'd have to break the glass, and standard window glass breaks into these massive, dangerous shards, unlike tempered glass in car windows that's designed to shatter into tiny cubes... \n\nBut, um... what were you asking? Oh. Yeah, even when I try to sit down and tinker with my electronics now, or put on a record, I just feel empty. None of it really does anything for me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the pipe bursting in your old apartment and the subsequent flood was the main trigger for how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess the flood was the start of it, yeah. But really, what brings me in is that I can't shake this feeling that I've just... ruined things for everyone. By falling behind at work, and losing control of the whole apartment situation. It's like I deserve whatever bad thing happens next. \n\nAnd you know, speaking of the apartment situation, when the pipe burst, the water damage wasn't just my unit. It leaked down into the apartment below mine. The guy down there, he had this massive collection of vintage comic books. I felt so bad. Comic books are notoriously difficult to restore once they get waterlogged. The paper they used back in the day, especially in the golden and silver ages, was this really cheap newsprint. It's highly acidic, which is why old comics turn yellow and brittle if they aren't stored in Mylar bags with acid-free backing boards. Mylar is incredible stuff, actually. It's a polyester film developed by DuPont in the 1950s. They use it for everything from food packaging to space blankets because it reflects heat so well. NASA used it extensively on the Apollo lunar modules. It looks like tin foil, but it's incredibly strong. \n\nBut, yeah... I just feel like everything is my fault. Like I'm just dragging everyone down at work because I can't keep up, and the whole apartment thing... I just feel like I've ruined it all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or make you feel slightly better?\n\nPatient: I know you asked me something similar earlier, and... honestly, the answer hasn't really changed. I really try to find those little things, but most days I just wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. It's like... the things that used to reset my brain just don't reach me anymore. I used to love putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with electronics... \n\nSpeaking of cooking, I used to make this amazing slow-cooked brisket. The secret is maintaining a perfectly consistent temperature, around 225 degrees, for like, twelve hours. A lot of people rush it, but if you do, the collagen in the meat doesn't break down into gelatin properly. Collagen is a fascinating protein, actually. It's the most abundant protein in the human body, making up our tendons, ligaments, and skin. When you heat it slowly in the presence of moisture, the triple-helix structure unwinds, which is what makes tough cuts of meat suddenly melt in your mouth. You really need a good digital thermometer with dual probes—one for the meat internal temp and one for the ambient smoker temp. The cheap analog ones that come built into most grills can be off by as much as 50 degrees because the bimetallic strips inside them degrade after a few heat cycles... \n\nBut yeah, like I was saying... even if I tried to cook or mess with my old electronics now, it just doesn't do anything for me. I just wake up feeling that same heavy sadness every day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I feel like we keep circling back to this, doc... but no, not really. Like I said, most days I just wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. I try to force myself to do the things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with my electronics—but they just don't reach me anymore. It's just blank. \n\nActually, when I was trying to listen to a record the other day to cheer myself up, I was looking at the turntable and thinking about how analog audio actually works. It's amazing when you really break it down. The stylus physically vibrates in those microscopic grooves, and a tiny magnet and coil inside the cartridge convert that kinetic energy into an electrical signal. Moving magnet cartridges are the standard, but moving coil ones have a much lower moving mass, which means they can track high frequencies so much better. The trade-off is they have a super low output voltage, so you need a dedicated phono pre-amp with a massive amount of gain just to hear it without raising the noise floor. If your grounding wire isn't perfectly secured to the chassis, you get this awful 60-hertz hum that just ruins the whole listening experience... \n\nBut, um... anyway. Yeah. I don't really enjoy any of it right now. I just can't seem to find pleasure in those things anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a specific plan for how you would hurt yourself if you decided to act on those thoughts of not waking up?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't have a plan. I wouldn't actually do anything to hurt myself. But I do catch myself thinking, you know, that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up. Like, if I just slipped away in my sleep, then nobody would have to deal with me anymore. I wouldn't be dragging anyone down. \n\nSpeaking of sleep, I was reading this article the other night when I couldn't drift off. It was about REM sleep and how the brain basically paralyzes the body so you don't act out your dreams. It's called REM atonia. The motor neurons in the spinal cord are actively inhibited by neurotransmitters like GABA and glycine. It's wild because sometimes that mechanism glitches, and people either act out violent dreams, which is REM sleep behavior disorder, or they wake up but the paralysis hasn't worn off yet, which is sleep paralysis. Sleep paralysis has been documented for centuries—it's where the whole myth of the incubus sitting on someone's chest comes from. The brain is awake, but the body is still locked down, and because the amygdala is highly active during REM, people often hallucinate these terrifying, shadowy figures in the room with them while they can't move a muscle...\n\nBut, um... anyway. No. No specific plan to hurt myself. Just... sometimes wishing I wouldn't wake up.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to find something positive, doctor, but... like I've been saying, the answer is still no. I just wake up most days with this flat, heavy sadness. I try, I really do. I try to look forward to the things that used to reset my brain—putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with my electronics—but they just don't reach me anymore. \n\nYou know, speaking of tinkering with electronics, I was actually looking at my soldering iron the other day. It's a Hakko FX-888D, which is a fantastic station for the price. The thermal recovery on it is incredible compared to those cheap pencil irons you plug directly into the wall. When you're soldering delicate components on a PCB, you need the tip to maintain a perfectly stable temperature, usually around 350 degrees Celsius for lead-free solder. If the iron loses heat too fast when it touches the copper pad, you end up with a cold solder joint, which looks dull and grainy and creates a high-resistance connection that can totally ruin the circuit. And you have to use a good flux, too. Rosin flux is the classic choice because it cleans the oxidation off the metal right as the heat is applied, allowing the solder to flow smoothly and create a perfect intermetallic bond...\n\nBut, sorry, I'm rambling again. The point is... no. Even looking at my tools, there's just nothing there. No small pleasures. Just that same heavy sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven't really stepped on a scale lately, to be honest. I think my clothes fit about the same, so probably not. I did actually buy one of those smart scales a couple of years ago, though, the ones that sync to your Wi-Fi and claim to measure your body fat using bioelectrical impedance analysis. It's a neat idea, but it's a pretty flawed metric for home use when you look at the tech. The scale sends a tiny, imperceptible electrical current up one bare foot and down the other to measure the resistance. Because fat contains less water than muscle, it resists the current more. But the thing is, electricity always takes the path of least resistance, so it's really only measuring the composition of your lower body. And your hydration levels completely skew the data anyway. If you just drink a large glass of water, your body fat percentage magically drops on the app. The actual weight measurement part is solid, though—they use these strain gauges in a Wheatstone bridge circuit that change their electrical resistance when the metal physically deforms under your weight... \n\nBut, um... sorry. I don't know why I got into all that. To answer your question, no. I haven't noticed any real changes in my weight.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I mentioned it a minute ago, but yeah. It's basically everything. I wake up most days with this... this flat, heavy sadness. And I try to do the things that used to reset my brain, you know? Putting on a record, cooking a nice meal, tinkering with my electronics. But they just don't reach me anymore. It's like the signal is blocked. \n\nSpeaking of blocked signals, I was troubleshooting a network issue for a client the other day. They were complaining about dropped packets and terrible latency on their Wi-Fi. Turns out they had their router sitting right next to a microwave and a cordless phone base station, both blasting interference on the 2.4 gigahertz band. It's a common mistake. The 2.4 band is super crowded—Bluetooth, baby monitors, you name it, they all use it. It has better range and penetrates walls better than the 5 gigahertz band, but the congestion causes massive packet loss. I had to explain to them how carrier-sense multiple access with collision avoidance works—basically, the devices have to listen to see if the channel is clear before they transmit, and if it's too noisy, they just keep waiting, which tanks your throughput. I ended up just moving them over to the 5 gigahertz band and setting up a mesh network to cover the dead zones...\n\nBut, um... yeah. To answer your question, those things I used to enjoy, like my electronics and music... they just don't bring me any pleasure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to find something, doctor, but... I feel like I keep saying the same thing. No. There really isn't anything. Most days I just wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. I try to do the things that used to reset my brain, you know? Like putting on a record, or cooking, or tinkering with my electronics. But they just don't reach me anymore. \n\nActually, speaking of records, I was trying to clean my turntable stylus yesterday. You really have to be careful with those. I use this specialized polymer gel pad—you just lower the tonearm so the diamond tip gently dips into the gel, and it pulls off all the microscopic dust and vinyl debris without putting any lateral stress on the cantilever. If you use a brush and accidentally sweep back to front instead of front to back, you can bend the cantilever, and then your tracking angle is completely ruined. And tracking angle is everything. If the stylus isn't sitting perfectly in the groove, you get this awful inner-groove distortion as the record plays closer to the center label, because the wavelengths of the high frequencies get physically compressed into a smaller circumference... \n\nBut, sorry. I'm doing it again. The answer is just no. I don't enjoy those things anymore. There's nothing I really find pleasure in right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles, Doc. I mean, yes. Like I've been saying, most days I wake up with this... this flat, heavy sadness. I try to do the things that used to reset my brain, you know? Putting on a record, cooking a nice meal, tinkering with my electronics. But they just don't reach me anymore. It's like the signal is blocked.\n\nSpeaking of blocked signals, did you know that the microwaves in your kitchen operate at the exact same frequency as most standard Wi-Fi routers? 2.4 gigahertz. It's because water molecules resonate at that frequency, which is how the microwave heats up your food. But the magnetron inside the microwave is basically a brute-force transmitter, so if the shielding on the door isn't perfect—and it almost never is on older models—it just leaks all this electromagnetic noise into your house. So every time you heat up a Hot Pocket, you're essentially jamming your own Wi-Fi network. I had a client once who couldn't figure out why his Zoom calls kept dropping every day at exactly 12:30 PM. Turned out his wife was just heating up her lunch in the kitchen right next to his home office. It's almost like my brain and body are running on a delayed connection, you know? \n\nLike high latency. It reminds me of this time I was dealing with a satellite internet setup for a client out in a rural area. Satellite is notorious for latency because the signal literally has to travel 22,000 miles up to space and back down again. You're dealing with the speed of light, which is fast, obviously, but over that distance, it introduces a physical delay of about 500 to 600 milliseconds. People think bandwidth and latency are the same thing, but they're completely different. You can have a massive pipe—huge bandwidth—but if the latency is high, everything feels sluggish. It's like having a ten-lane highway, but the speed limit is five miles an hour. Every time you click a link, you have to wait for the request to go to space, hit the server, go back to space, and come back to your dish. It makes real-time applications like VoIP or online gaming completely impossible...\n\nBut, um... yeah. The delay. My body feels like that satellite connection right now. Like, 90% of the time, the user just hasn't restarted their machine in three weeks, and their RAM is completely bogged down with background processes. People don't realize that when you just close the lid of your laptop, it's not actually turning off. It goes into sleep mode, which keeps the current state saved in the memory. So if you have a memory leak from a poorly coded application, it just keeps eating up more and more resources over time until the whole system starts crawling. That's why the first thing we always ask is, \"Have you tried turning it off and on again?\" It sounds like a joke, but it actually forces the operating system to clear the volatile memory and reload everything from scratch. It's called a hard power cycle. Sometimes you even have to unplug the power cable and hold the power button down for thirty seconds to discharge the capacitors on the motherboard, otherwise the residual charge can keep the system in a locked state...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, I can still concentrate fine when I need to. If the iron loses heat too fast when it touches the copper pad, you end up with a cold solder joint, which looks dull and grainy and creates a high-resistance connection that can totally ruin the circuit. And you have to use a good flux, too. Rosin flux is the classic choice because it cleans the oxidation off the metal right as the heat is applied, allowing the solder to flow smoothly and create a perfect intermetallic bond...\n\nBut, sorry, I'm rambling again. The point is... no. Even looking at my tools, there's just nothing there. No small pleasures. Just that same heavy sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven't really stepped on a scale lately, to be honest. I think my clothes fit about the same, so probably not. I did actually buy one of those smart scales a couple of years ago, though, the ones that sync to your Wi-Fi and claim to measure your body fat using bioelectrical impedance analysis. It's a neat idea, but it's a pretty flawed metric for home use when you look at the tech. The scale sends a tiny, imperceptible electrical current up one bare foot and down the other to measure the resistance. Because fat contains less water than muscle, it resists the current more. But the thing is, electricity always takes the path of least resistance, so it's really only measuring the composition of your lower body. And your hydration levels completely skew the data anyway. If you just drink a large glass of water, your body fat percentage magically drops on the app. The actual weight measurement part is solid, though—they use these strain gauges in a Wheatstone bridge circuit that change their electrical resistance when the metal physically deforms under your weight... \n\nBut, um... sorry. I don't know why I got into all that. To answer your question, no. I haven't noticed any real changes in my weight.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I mentioned it a minute ago, but yeah. It's basically everything. I wake up most days with this... this flat, heavy sadness. And I try to do the things that used to reset my brain, you know? Putting on a record, cooking a nice meal, tinkering with my electronics. But they just don't reach me anymore. It's like the signal is blocked. \n\nSpeaking of blocked signals, I was troubleshooting a network issue for a client the other day. They were complaining about dropped packets and terrible latency on their Wi-Fi. Turns out they had their router sitting right next to a microwave and a cordless phone base station, both blasting interference on the 2.4 gigahertz band. It's a common mistake. The 2.4 band is super crowded—Bluetooth, baby monitors, you name it, they all use it. It has better range and penetrates walls better than the 5 gigahertz band, but the congestion causes massive packet loss. I had to explain to them how carrier-sense multiple access with collision avoidance works—basically, the devices have to listen to see if the channel is clear before they transmit, and if it's too noisy, they just keep waiting, which tanks your throughput. I ended up just moving them over to the 5 gigahertz band and setting up a mesh network to cover the dead zones...\n\nBut, um... yeah. To answer your question, those things I used to enjoy, like my electronics and music... they just don't bring me any pleasure anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to find something, doctor, but... I feel like I keep saying the same thing. No. There really isn't anything. Most days I just wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. I try to do the things that used to reset my brain, you know? Like putting on a record, or cooking, or tinkering with my electronics. But they just don't reach me anymore. \n\nActually, speaking of records, I was trying to clean my turntable stylus yesterday. You really have to be careful with those. I use this specialized polymer gel pad—you just lower the tonearm so the diamond tip gently dips into the gel, and it pulls off all the microscopic dust and vinyl debris without putting any lateral stress on the cantilever. If you use a brush and accidentally sweep back to front instead of front to back, you can bend the cantilever, and then your tracking angle is completely ruined. And tracking angle is everything. If the stylus isn't sitting perfectly in the groove, you get this awful inner-groove distortion as the record plays closer to the center label, because the wavelengths of the high frequencies get physically compressed into a smaller circumference... \n\nBut, sorry. I'm doing it again. The answer is just no. I don't enjoy those things anymore. There's nothing I really find pleasure in right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles, Doc. I mean, yes. Like I've been saying, most days I wake up with this... this flat, heavy sadness. I try to do the things that used to reset my brain, you know? Putting on a record, cooking a nice meal, tinkering with my electronics. But they just don't reach me anymore. It's like the signal is blocked.\n\nSpeaking of blocked signals, did you know that the microwaves in your kitchen operate at the exact same frequency as most standard Wi-Fi routers? 2.4 gigahertz. It's because water molecules resonate at that frequency, which is how the microwave heats up your food. But the magnetron inside the microwave is basically a brute-force transmitter, so if the shielding on the door isn't perfect—and it almost never is on older models—it just leaks all this electromagnetic noise into your house. So every time you heat up a Hot Pocket, you're essentially jamming your own Wi-Fi network. I had a client once who couldn't figure out why his Zoom calls kept dropping every day at exactly 12:30 PM. Turned out his wife was just heating up her lunch in the kitchen right next to his home office. I had to explain the whole concept of electromagnetic interference and move his router to the 5 gigahertz band...\n\nBut, uh... sorry. I lost my train of thought. Yeah, I've lost interest in everything. Nothing really helps anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. It's most obvious at work. I'll just be sitting there, and I notice my hands just hovering over the keyboard. It takes me so much longer to answer even the simplest support tickets. It's almost like my brain and body are running on a delayed connection, you know? \n\nLike high latency. It reminds me of this time I was dealing with a satellite internet setup for a client out in a rural area. Satellite is notorious for latency because the signal literally has to travel 22,000 miles up to space and back down again. You're dealing with the speed of light, which is fast, obviously, but over that distance, it introduces a physical delay of about 500 to 600 milliseconds. People think bandwidth and latency are the same thing, but they're completely different. You can have a massive pipe—huge bandwidth—but if the latency is high, everything feels sluggish. It's like having a ten-lane highway, but the speed limit is five miles an hour. Every time you click a link, you have to wait for the request to go to space, hit the server, go back to space, and come back to your dish. It makes real-time applications like VoIP or online gaming completely impossible...\n\nBut, um... yeah. The delay. My body feels like that satellite connection right now. Just... slow to respond.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having difficulty concentrating or making decisions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any issues with concentrating or making decisions. I mean, my job is basically just making decisions all day, figuring out what's wrong with people's computers. \n\nUsually, it's pretty straightforward. Like, 90% of the time, the user just hasn't restarted their machine in three weeks, and their RAM is completely bogged down with background processes. People don't realize that when you just close the lid of your laptop, it's not actually turning off. It goes into sleep mode, which keeps the current state saved in the memory. So if you have a memory leak from a poorly coded application, it just keeps eating up more and more resources over time until the whole system starts crawling. That's why the first thing we always ask is, \"Have you tried turning it off and on again?\" It sounds like a joke, but it actually forces the operating system to clear the volatile memory and reload everything from scratch. It's called a hard power cycle. Sometimes you even have to unplug the power cable and hold the power button down for thirty seconds to discharge the capacitors on the motherboard, otherwise the residual charge can keep the system in a locked state...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, I can still concentrate fine when I need to. I haven't had any trouble with that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, way longer than two weeks. I mean, we kind of touched on this earlier, but this has been going on for about a year and two months. It all really started when a pipe burst in my old place, which forced me into this much smaller apartment, and all my daily routines just completely fell apart. 

Speaking of the pipe bursting, water damage is just an absolute nightmare for electronics. I lost a really good home server setup in that mess. People always think it's the water itself that destroys the hardware, but pure distilled water is actually a pretty good insulator. It's the dissolved minerals in tap water—the calcium, the magnesium, the salt—that make it highly conductive. So when water hits a live motherboard, those minerals bridge the electrical pathways that aren't supposed to be connected and cause an immediate short circuit. And even if you unplug it in time and manage to dry it out, those same minerals get left behind on the board and start this process called galvanic corrosion. I have clients all the time who spill water on their laptops, and they think because they dried it off and it turned back on, they're in the clear. They always try the rice trick, which is a complete myth, by the way—it just gets starch and dust packed into your charging ports. But anyway, that mineral residue is just sitting there, slowly eating away at the copper traces on the printed circuit board. Give it three weeks, and the whole logic board is basically a paperweight. I always tell them you have to open it up and displace the water with 99 percent isopropyl alcohol, otherwise... 

Wait, sorry. I'm rambling again. What was the question? Oh, the timeline. Yeah, a year and two months of this. Since I had to move.

Sometimes I just think... if I just stayed asleep, it would solve a lot of problems for everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending time with friends and family lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have been. The building was built in the 1920s, and they still had the original galvanized steel plumbing in some of the walls. Galvanized pipes are just a ticking time bomb because they corrode from the inside out. You can't even tell how bad it is just by looking at the outside of the pipe until it finally gives way. The water pressure in that neighborhood was always kind of erratic anyway, probably because the city's main lines are just as old. I remember reading that some cities still have wooden water mains in use from the 1800s. Can you believe that? Wood! They used hollowed-out logs bound with steel bands. It's actually fascinating from an engineering standpoint how they managed to make that watertight back then without modern sealants. \n\nBut anyway, the pipe burst and completely flooded my place. Most days I just wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. It's like the things that used to reset me—you know, putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with electronics—they just don't reach me anymore. \n\nI used to spend hours fixing up old audio equipment. I have this vintage receiver from the 70s, and the build quality on those old units is just incredible. They used these massive capacitors and real wood casing, not like the cheap injection-molded plastic stuff today. The problem with them, though, is the potentiometers get dusty over time, so when you turn the volume dial, you get this awful scratchy static sound. You have to open it up and spray this specific chemical contact cleaner into the tiny gaps in the dial housing. It's actually a pretty harsh solvent, so you have to do it in a well-ventilated area, which is hard in my new apartment because the windows are completely painted shut. \n\nI really don't know why landlords do that, just slathering layer after layer of cheap white latex paint over the window sashes until they're basically glued to the frame. It's a huge fire hazard, honestly. Comic books are notoriously difficult to restore once they get waterlogged. The paper they used back in the day, especially in the golden and silver ages, was this really cheap newsprint. It's highly acidic, which is why old comics turn yellow and brittle if they aren't stored in Mylar bags with acid-free backing boards. Mylar is incredible stuff, actually. It's a polyester film developed by DuPont in the 1950s. They use it for everything from food packaging to space blankets because it reflects heat so well. NASA used it extensively on the Apollo lunar modules. It looks like tin foil, but it's incredibly strong. \n\nBut, yeah... I just feel like everything is my fault. Like I'm just dragging everyone down at work because I can't keep up, and the whole apartment thing... I used to love putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with electronics... \n\nSpeaking of cooking, I used to make this amazing slow-cooked brisket. The secret is maintaining a perfectly consistent temperature, around 225 degrees, for like, twelve hours. A lot of people rush it, but if you do, the collagen in the meat doesn't break down into gelatin properly. Collagen is a fascinating protein, actually. It's the most abundant protein in the human body, making up our tendons, ligaments, and skin. When you heat it slowly in the presence of moisture, the triple-helix structure unwinds, which is what makes tough cuts of meat suddenly melt in your mouth. You really need a good digital thermometer with dual probes—one for the meat internal temp and one for the ambient smoker temp. The cheap analog ones that come built into most grills can be off by as much as 50 degrees because the bimetallic strips inside them degrade after a few heat cycles... \n\nBut yeah, like I was saying... even if I tried to cook or mess with my old electronics now, it just doesn't do anything for me. I try to force myself to do the things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with my electronics—but they just don't reach me anymore. It's just blank. \n\nActually, when I was trying to listen to a record the other day to cheer myself up, I was looking at the turntable and thinking about how analog audio actually works. It's amazing when you really break it down. The stylus physically vibrates in those microscopic grooves, and a tiny magnet and coil inside the cartridge convert that kinetic energy into an electrical signal. Moving magnet cartridges are the standard, but moving coil ones have a much lower moving mass, which means they can track high frequencies so much better. The trade-off is they have a super low output voltage, so you need a dedicated phono pre-amp with a massive amount of gain just to hear it without raising the noise floor. If your grounding wire isn't perfectly secured to the chassis, you get this awful 60-hertz hum that just ruins the whole listening experience... \n\nBut, um... anyway. Yeah. Like, if I just slipped away in my sleep, then nobody would have to deal with me anymore. I wouldn't be dragging anyone down. \n\nSpeaking of sleep, I was reading this article the other night when I couldn't drift off. It was about REM sleep and how the brain basically paralyzes the body so you don't act out your dreams. It's called REM atonia. The motor neurons in the spinal cord are actively inhibited by neurotransmitters like GABA and glycine. It's wild because sometimes that mechanism glitches, and people either act out violent dreams, which is REM sleep behavior disorder, or they wake up but the paralysis hasn't worn off yet, which is sleep paralysis. Sleep paralysis has been documented for centuries—it's where the whole myth of the incubus sitting on someone's chest comes from. The brain is awake, but the body is still locked down, and because the amygdala is highly active during REM, people often hallucinate these terrifying, shadowy figures in the room with them while they can't move a muscle...\n\nBut, um... anyway. It's a Hakko FX-888D, which is a fantastic station for the price. The thermal recovery on it is incredible compared to those cheap pencil irons you plug directly into the wall. When you're soldering delicate components on a PCB, you need the tip to maintain a perfectly stable temperature, usually around 350 degrees Celsius for lead-free solder. If the iron loses heat too fast when it touches the copper pad, you end up with a cold solder joint, which looks dull and grainy and creates a high-resistance connection that can totally ruin the circuit. And you have to use a good flux, too. Rosin flux is the classic choice because it cleans the oxidation off the metal right as the heat is applied, allowing the solder to flow smoothly and create a perfect intermetallic bond...\n\nBut, sorry, I'm rambling again. It's a neat idea, but it's a pretty flawed metric for home use when you look at the tech. The scale sends a tiny, imperceptible electrical current up one bare foot and down the other to measure the resistance. Because fat contains less water than muscle, it resists the current more. But the thing is, electricity always takes the path of least resistance, so it's really only measuring the composition of your lower body. And your hydration levels completely skew the data anyway. If you just drink a large glass of water, your body fat percentage magically drops on the app. The actual weight measurement part is solid, though—they use these strain gauges in a Wheatstone bridge circuit that change their electrical resistance when the metal physically deforms under your weight... \n\nBut, um... sorry. The 2.4 band is super crowded—Bluetooth, baby monitors, you name it, they all use it. It has better range and penetrates walls better than the 5 gigahertz band, but the congestion causes massive packet loss. I had to explain to them how carrier-sense multiple access with collision avoidance works—basically, the devices have to listen to see if the channel is clear before they transmit, and if it's too noisy, they just keep waiting, which tanks your throughput. I ended up just moving them over to the 5 gigahertz band and setting up a mesh network to cover the dead zones...\n\nBut, um... yeah. If you use a brush and accidentally sweep back to front instead of front to back, you can bend the cantilever, and then your tracking angle is completely ruined. And tracking angle is everything. If the stylus isn't sitting perfectly in the groove, you get this awful inner-groove distortion as the record plays closer to the center label, because the wavelengths of the high frequencies get physically compressed into a smaller circumference... \n\nBut, sorry. It's like the signal is blocked.\n\nSpeaking of blocked signals, did you know that the microwaves in your kitchen operate at the exact same frequency as most standard Wi-Fi routers? 2.4 gigahertz. You're dealing with the speed of light, which is fast, obviously, but over that distance, it introduces a physical delay of about 500 to 600 milliseconds. People think bandwidth and latency are the same thing, but they're completely different. You can have a massive pipe—huge bandwidth—but if the latency is high, everything feels sluggish. It's like having a ten-lane highway, but the speed limit is five miles an hour. Like, 90% of the time, the user just hasn't restarted their machine in three weeks, and their RAM is completely bogged down with background processes. People don't realize that when you just close the lid of your laptop, it's not actually turning off. It goes into sleep mode, which keeps the current state saved in the memory. So if you have a memory leak from a poorly coded application, it just keeps eating up more and more resources over time until the whole system starts crawling. That's why the first thing we always ask is, \"Have you tried turning it off and on again?\" It sounds like a joke, but it actually forces the operating system to clear the volatile memory and reload everything from scratch. It's called a hard power cycle. Sometimes you even have to unplug the power cable and hold the power button down for thirty seconds to discharge the capacitors on the motherboard, otherwise the residual charge can keep the system in a locked state...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, I can still concentrate fine when I need to. I lost a really good home server setup in that mess. People always think it's the water itself that destroys the hardware, but pure distilled water is actually a pretty good insulator. It's the dissolved minerals in tap water—the calcium, the magnesium, the salt—that make it highly conductive. So when water hits a live motherboard, those minerals bridge the electrical pathways that aren't supposed to be connected and cause an immediate short circuit. And even if you unplug it in time and manage to dry it out, those same minerals get left behind on the board and start this process called galvanic corrosion. I have clients all the time who spill water on their laptops, and they think because they dried it off and it turned back on, they're in the clear. They always try the rice trick, which is a complete myth, by the way—it just gets starch and dust packed into your charging ports. But anyway, that mineral residue is just sitting there, slowly eating away at the copper traces on the printed circuit board. The water pressure in that neighborhood was always kind of erratic anyway, probably because the city's main lines are just as old. I remember reading that some cities still have wooden water mains in use from the 1800s. Can you believe that? Wood! They used hollowed-out logs bound with steel bands. It's actually fascinating from an engineering standpoint how they managed to make that watertight back then without modern sealants. \n\nBut anyway, the pipe burst and completely flooded my place. I lost so much stuff, including my favorite mechanical keyboard. I had to move into this much smaller apartment across town, and... I don't know, my whole routine just fell apart after that. I haven't been the same since. That's when the heaviness really started.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I try to, but... not really. Most days I just wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. It's like the things that used to reset me—you know, putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with electronics—they just don't reach me anymore. \n\nI used to spend hours fixing up old audio equipment. I have this vintage receiver from the 70s, and the build quality on those old units is just incredible. They used these massive capacitors and real wood casing, not like the cheap injection-molded plastic stuff today. The problem with them, though, is the potentiometers get dusty over time, so when you turn the volume dial, you get this awful scratchy static sound. You have to open it up and spray this specific chemical contact cleaner into the tiny gaps in the dial housing. It's actually a pretty harsh solvent, so you have to do it in a well-ventilated area, which is hard in my new apartment because the windows are completely painted shut. \n\nI really don't know why landlords do that, just slathering layer after layer of cheap white latex paint over the window sashes until they're basically glued to the frame. It's a huge fire hazard, honestly. If there was an emergency, you'd have to break the glass, and standard window glass breaks into these massive, dangerous shards, unlike tempered glass in car windows that's designed to shatter into tiny cubes... \n\nBut, um... what were you asking? Oh. Yeah, even when I try to sit down and tinker with my electronics now, or put on a record, I just feel empty. None of it really does anything for me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the pipe bursting in your old apartment and the subsequent flood was the main trigger for how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess the flood was the start of it, yeah. But really, what brings me in is that I can't shake this feeling that I've just... ruined things for everyone. By falling behind at work, and losing control of the whole apartment situation. It's like I deserve whatever bad thing happens next. \n\nAnd you know, speaking of the apartment situation, when the pipe burst, the water damage wasn't just my unit. It leaked down into the apartment below mine. The guy down there, he had this massive collection of vintage comic books. I felt so bad. Comic books are notoriously difficult to restore once they get waterlogged. The paper they used back in the day, especially in the golden and silver ages, was this really cheap newsprint. It's highly acidic, which is why old comics turn yellow and brittle if they aren't stored in Mylar bags with acid-free backing boards. Mylar is incredible stuff, actually. It's a polyester film developed by DuPont in the 1950s. They use it for everything from food packaging to space blankets because it reflects heat so well. NASA used it extensively on the Apollo lunar modules. It looks like tin foil, but it's incredibly strong. \n\nBut, yeah... I just feel like everything is my fault. Like I'm just dragging everyone down at work because I can't keep up, and the whole apartment thing... I just feel like I've ruined it all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or make you feel slightly better?\n\nPatient: I know you asked me something similar earlier, and... honestly, the answer hasn't really changed. I really try to find those little things, but most days I just wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. It's like... the things that used to reset my brain just don't reach me anymore. I used to love putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with electronics... \n\nSpeaking of cooking, I used to make this amazing slow-cooked brisket. The secret is maintaining a perfectly consistent temperature, around 225 degrees, for like, twelve hours. A lot of people rush it, but if you do, the collagen in the meat doesn't break down into gelatin properly. Collagen is a fascinating protein, actually. It's the most abundant protein in the human body, making up our tendons, ligaments, and skin. When you heat it slowly in the presence of moisture, the triple-helix structure unwinds, which is what makes tough cuts of meat suddenly melt in your mouth. You really need a good digital thermometer with dual probes—one for the meat internal temp and one for the ambient smoker temp. The cheap analog ones that come built into most grills can be off by as much as 50 degrees because the bimetallic strips inside them degrade after a few heat cycles... \n\nBut yeah, like I was saying... even if I tried to cook or mess with my old electronics now, it just doesn't do anything for me. I just wake up feeling that same heavy sadness every day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I feel like we keep circling back to this, doc... but no, not really. Like I said, most days I just wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. I try to force myself to do the things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with my electronics—but they just don't reach me anymore. It's just blank. \n\nActually, when I was trying to listen to a record the other day to cheer myself up, I was looking at the turntable and thinking about how analog audio actually works. It's amazing when you really break it down. The stylus physically vibrates in those microscopic grooves, and a tiny magnet and coil inside the cartridge convert that kinetic energy into an electrical signal. Moving magnet cartridges are the standard, but moving coil ones have a much lower moving mass, which means they can track high frequencies so much better. The trade-off is they have a super low output voltage, so you need a dedicated phono pre-amp with a massive amount of gain just to hear it without raising the noise floor. If your grounding wire isn't perfectly secured to the chassis, you get this awful 60-hertz hum that just ruins the whole listening experience... \n\nBut, um... anyway. Yeah. I don't really enjoy any of it right now. I just can't seem to find pleasure in those things anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a specific plan for how you would hurt yourself if you decided to act on those thoughts of not waking up?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't have a plan. I wouldn't actually do anything to hurt myself. But I do catch myself thinking, you know, that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up. Like, if I just slipped away in my sleep, then nobody would have to deal with me anymore. I wouldn't be dragging anyone down. \n\nSpeaking of sleep, I was reading this article the other night when I couldn't drift off. It was about REM sleep and how the brain basically paralyzes the body so you don't act out your dreams. It's called REM atonia. The motor neurons in the spinal cord are actively inhibited by neurotransmitters like GABA and glycine. It's wild because sometimes that mechanism glitches, and people either act out violent dreams, which is REM sleep behavior disorder, or they wake up but the paralysis hasn't worn off yet, which is sleep paralysis. Sleep paralysis has been documented for centuries—it's where the whole myth of the incubus sitting on someone's chest comes from. The brain is awake, but the body is still locked down, and because the amygdala is highly active during REM, people often hallucinate these terrifying, shadowy figures in the room with them while they can't move a muscle...\n\nBut, um... anyway. No. No specific plan to hurt myself. Just... sometimes wishing I wouldn't wake up.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to find something positive, doctor, but... like I've been saying, the answer is still no. I try to look forward to the things that used to reset my brain—putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with my electronics—but they just don't reach me anymore. \n\nYou know, speaking of tinkering with electronics, I was actually looking at my soldering iron the other day. It's a Hakko FX-888D, which is a fantastic station for the price. The thermal recovery on it is incredible compared to those cheap pencil irons you plug directly into the wall. When you're soldering delicate components on a PCB, you need the tip to maintain a perfectly stable temperature, usually around 350 degrees Celsius for lead-free solder. If the iron loses heat too fast when it touches the copper pad, you end up with a cold solder joint, which looks dull and grainy and creates a high-resistance connection that can totally ruin the circuit. And you have to use a good flux, too. Rosin flux is the classic choice because it cleans the oxidation off the metal right as the heat is applied, allowing the solder to flow smoothly and create a perfect intermetallic bond...\n\nBut, sorry, I'm rambling again. The point is... no. Even looking at my tools, there's just nothing there. No small pleasures. Just that same heavy sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven't really stepped on a scale lately, to be honest. I think my clothes fit about the same, so probably not. I did actually buy one of those smart scales a couple of years ago, though, the ones that sync to your Wi-Fi and claim to measure your body fat using bioelectrical impedance analysis. It's a neat idea, but it's a pretty flawed metric for home use when you look at the tech. The scale sends a tiny, imperceptible electrical current up one bare foot and down the other to measure the resistance. Because fat contains less water than muscle, it resists the current more. But the thing is, electricity always takes the path of least resistance, so it's really only measuring the composition of your lower body. And your hydration levels completely skew the data anyway. If you just drink a large glass of water, your body fat percentage magically drops on the app. The actual weight measurement part is solid, though—they use these strain gauges in a Wheatstone bridge circuit that change their electrical resistance when the metal physically deforms under your weight... \n\nBut, um... sorry. Putting on a record, cooking a nice meal, tinkering with my electronics. But they just don't reach me anymore. It's like the signal is blocked. \n\nSpeaking of blocked signals, I was troubleshooting a network issue for a client the other day. They were complaining about dropped packets and terrible latency on their Wi-Fi. Turns out they had their router sitting right next to a microwave and a cordless phone base station, both blasting interference on the 2.4 gigahertz band. It's a common mistake. The 2.4 band is super crowded—Bluetooth, baby monitors, you name it, they all use it. It has better range and penetrates walls better than the 5 gigahertz band, but the congestion causes massive packet loss. I had to explain to them how carrier-sense multiple access with collision avoidance works—basically, the devices have to listen to see if the channel is clear before they transmit, and if it's too noisy, they just keep waiting, which tanks your throughput. I ended up just moving them over to the 5 gigahertz band and setting up a mesh network to cover the dead zones...\n\nBut, um... yeah. You really have to be careful with those. I use this specialized polymer gel pad—you just lower the tonearm so the diamond tip gently dips into the gel, and it pulls off all the microscopic dust and vinyl debris without putting any lateral stress on the cantilever. If you use a brush and accidentally sweep back to front instead of front to back, you can bend the cantilever, and then your tracking angle is completely ruined. And tracking angle is everything. If the stylus isn't sitting perfectly in the groove, you get this awful inner-groove distortion as the record plays closer to the center label, because the wavelengths of the high frequencies get physically compressed into a smaller circumference... \n\nBut, sorry. It's like the signal is blocked.\n\nSpeaking of blocked signals, did you know that the microwaves in your kitchen operate at the exact same frequency as most standard Wi-Fi routers? 2.4 gigahertz. It's because water molecules resonate at that frequency, which is how the microwave heats up your food. But the magnetron inside the microwave is basically a brute-force transmitter, so if the shielding on the door isn't perfect—and it almost never is on older models—it just leaks all this electromagnetic noise into your house. So every time you heat up a Hot Pocket, you're essentially jamming your own Wi-Fi network. I had a client once who couldn't figure out why his Zoom calls kept dropping every day at exactly 12:30 PM. It reminds me of this time I was dealing with a satellite internet setup for a client out in a rural area. Satellite is notorious for latency because the signal literally has to travel 22,000 miles up to space and back down again. You're dealing with the speed of light, which is fast, obviously, but over that distance, it introduces a physical delay of about 500 to 600 milliseconds. People think bandwidth and latency are the same thing, but they're completely different. You can have a massive pipe—huge bandwidth—but if the latency is high, everything feels sluggish. It's like having a ten-lane highway, but the speed limit is five miles an hour. Every time you click a link, you have to wait for the request to go to space, hit the server, go back to space, and come back to your dish. I mean, my job is basically just making decisions all day, figuring out what's wrong with people's computers. \n\nUsually, it's pretty straightforward. Like, 90% of the time, the user just hasn't restarted their machine in three weeks, and their RAM is completely bogged down with background processes. People don't realize that when you just close the lid of your laptop, it's not actually turning off. It goes into sleep mode, which keeps the current state saved in the memory. So if you have a memory leak from a poorly coded application, it just keeps eating up more and more resources over time until the whole system starts crawling. That's why the first thing we always ask is, \"Have you tried turning it off and on again?\" It sounds like a joke, but it actually forces the operating system to clear the volatile memory and reload everything from scratch. It's called a hard power cycle. Sometimes you even have to unplug the power cable and hold the power button down for thirty seconds to discharge the capacitors on the motherboard, otherwise the residual charge can keep the system in a locked state...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, I can still concentrate fine when I need to. I lost a really good home server setup in that mess. People always think it's the water itself that destroys the hardware, but pure distilled water is actually a pretty good insulator. It's the dissolved minerals in tap water—the calcium, the magnesium, the salt—that make it highly conductive. So when water hits a live motherboard, those minerals bridge the electrical pathways that aren't supposed to be connected and cause an immediate short circuit. And even if you unplug it in time and manage to dry it out, those same minerals get left behind on the board and start this process called galvanic corrosion. I have clients all the time who spill water on their laptops, and they think because they dried it off and it turned back on, they're in the clear. They always try the rice trick, which is a complete myth, by the way—it just gets starch and dust packed into your charging ports. But anyway, that mineral residue is just sitting there, slowly eating away at the copper traces on the printed circuit board. Give it three weeks, and the whole logic board is basically a paperweight. I always tell them you have to open it up and displace the water with 99 percent isopropyl alcohol, otherwise... \n\nWait, sorry. I'm rambling again. What was the question? Oh, the timeline. Yeah, a year and two months of this. Since I had to move."
